After substituting <a href="#polar:solved-kernels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="polar:solved-kernels">[polar:solved-kernels]</a>, let $g_i(\xi)$ denote the bilinear equation $b^{\mathsf T}B_i p$. For any constant ambient direction $w$, differentiation of $S$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \partial_w S
 &=\partial_w a-(\partial_w\beta)E^{-1}\gamma
       +\beta E^{-1}(\partial_w E)E^{-1}\gamma
       -\beta E^{-1}(\partial_w\gamma)\\
 &=(1,-\beta E^{-1})(\partial_w A)
                 \binom{1}{-E^{-1}\gamma}.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $g_i=(\partial_{w_i}S)\circ\Psi$. In particular this computation does not identify an ambient derivative with a derivative along the slice. If $f=Q\circ\Psi$ and $q_i=(\partial_{w_i}Q)\circ\Psi$, differentiating <a href="#polar:schur-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="polar:schur-determinant">[polar:schur-determinant]</a> in the ambient space yields <a id="polar:reduced-system"></a>$$\label{polar:reduced-system}
 \begin{gathered}
 f=c\,s,\qquad q_i=c\,g_i+c_i s,\\
 c=\sigma(\det E)\circ\Psi\ne0,\qquad
 c_i=\sigma(\partial_{w_i}\det E)\circ\Psi.
 \end{gathered}$$ The original equation vector $(f,q_1,\ldots,q_h)$ and the reduced vector $(s,g_1,\ldots,g_h)$ therefore differ by a triangular holomorphic matrix with diagonal entries $c$. At a common zero their Jacobians differ by the value of that invertible matrix. The original zero was simple, so the reduced Jacobian is invertible.

Finally, a tangent vector annihilated by the full kernel-system Jacobian first satisfies the differentials of the eliminated equations. Their invertible auxiliary block determines its $(u,v)$ component from its $\xi$ component. The remaining equations and the invertible reduced Jacobian force that $\xi$ component to be zero, and hence the auxiliary component is zero as well. The full square Jacobian is invertible. All $\mu$ selected lifts are thus distinct simple zeros of <a href="#polar:kernel-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="polar:kernel-system">[polar:kernel-system]</a>.

*Count the selected lifts.* The three groups of equations in <a href="#polar:kernel-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="polar:kernel-system">[polar:kernel-system]</a> have multidegrees and multiplicities $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{equations}&\text{multidegree}&\text{number}\\ \hline
 A^{\mathrm{hom}} p &(1,1,0)&n\\
 (b^{\mathsf T}A^{\mathrm{hom}})_j, j\ge2 &(1,0,1)&n-1\\
 b^{\mathsf T}B_i p &(0,1,1)&h.
 \end{array}$$ Lemma <a href="#polar:product-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="polar:product-count">22</a> gives the first inequality in <a href="#polar:ambient-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="polar:ambient-coefficient">[polar:ambient-coefficient]</a>, regardless of other zeros or components. The product has nonnegative coefficients. Setting $U=V=1$ sums the coefficients with the same $H$-exponent, and hence bounds the particular coefficient by $$[H^{h+1}]\,2^h(H+1)^{2n-1}
       =2^h\binom{2n-1}{h+1}.$$ Since $\mu>0$, we must have $h+1\le2n-1$. For integers $1\le b\le a$, $$\binom ab\le\frac{a^b}{b!}\le\left(\frac{ea}{b}\right)^b;$$ the last bound follows from $\log(b!)\ge\int_1^b\log t\,dt\ge b\log b-b$. Taking $(h+1)$st roots gives $$\mu^{1/(h+1)}
 \le2^{h/(h+1)}\frac{e(2n-1)}{h+1}
 \le\frac{4en}{h+1},$$ which is <a href="#polar:ambient-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="polar:ambient-size">[polar:ambient-size]</a>. No comparison between $n$ and the number $N$ of ambient variables, or between $n$ and $h+2$, was assumed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The smooth-hypersurface lower bound

We have proved that a fixed finite set of simple polar intersections persists under arbitrary affine determinant approximation, and that its kernel lifts satisfy the multidegree bound. We now use the certificate provided by the smooth hypersurface.

<details id="proof-stmt-polar-ambient-bound-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-polar-ambient-bound</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:smooth-border" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:smooth-border">4</a>.* Let $n$ be any size for which $G$ is a coefficientwise limit of affine $n\times n$ determinants. Choose the directions and $L_0$ from Lemma <a href="#polar:simple-points" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="polar:simple-points">21</a>. The affine hyperplane $L_0=1$ identifies with the projective chart $L_0\ne0$ and contains representatives of all $\mu=r(r-1)^{d-2}$ selected points. Parametrize it by an affine isomorphism $$\Psi:\mathbb C^{d-1}\longrightarrow\{x\in\mathbb C^d:L_0(x)=1\}.$$ Restricting the homogeneous equations to this hyperplane is precisely their dehomogenization in that projective chart. Thus the equations $$G\circ\Psi=0,\qquad
 (\partial_{w_i}G)\circ\Psi=0\quad(1\le i\le d-2)$$ have $\mu$ distinct simple affine solutions. The directions remain constant vectors in $\mathbb C^d$; they need not be tangent to $L_0=1$. Apply Proposition <a href="#polar:ambient-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="polar:ambient-bound">23</a> with $N=d$, $h=d-2$, and $P=G$. It gives $$n\ge\frac{d-1}{4e}
       \bigl(r(r-1)^{d-2}\bigr)^{1/(d-1)}
   \ge\frac{(r-1)(d-1)}{4e},$$ where the last inequality uses $r\ge r-1>0$. This holds for every admissible size $n$, so taking the minimum proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### The permanent lower bound and its consequences

Proposition <a href="#prop:smooth-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:smooth-projection">20</a> supplies a smooth form with an exact affine permanent projection. Combining it with Theorem <a href="#thm:smooth-border" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:smooth-border">4</a> proves the main lower bound.

<details id="proof-stmt-polar-ambient-bound-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-polar-ambient-bound</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Let $m\ge1408$ be an integer and put $k=\lfloor m/11\rfloor$. Then $k\ge128$. With $q=\lfloor(k-1)/3\rfloor$, we have $q\ge0$ and $$m\ge11k\ge11k-2q.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:smooth-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:smooth-projection">20</a> supplies a nonzero homogeneous form $G$ of degree $r$ in $d$ variables, with smooth projective zero set, and a fixed affine map $\Phi_m$ such that $G=\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_m\circ\Phi_m$. Lemma <a href="#lem:affine-substitution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affine-substitution">2</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:smooth-border" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:smooth-border">4</a> give $$\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_m)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(G)
 \ge\frac{(r-1)(d-1)}{4e}
 \ge\frac{k^3}{3200e},$$ using the two scale bounds in Proposition <a href="#prop:smooth-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:smooth-projection">20</a>. The affine substitution is fixed before any determinant approximation is chosen, so this step applies to the unrestricted coefficientwise border model.

Finally, $$k\ge\frac{m}{11}-1\ge\frac{m}{12},$$ where the last inequality holds for $m\ge132$. Therefore $$\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_m)\ge\frac{m^3}{5\,529\,600e},$$ which proves the stated choices of $c$ and $m_0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Determinantal complexity and branching programs

The border lower bound gives the same estimate for exact determinant representations. A direct determinant construction also transfers it to algebraic branching programs and their fixed-budget limits.

<a id="stmt-cor-exact-complexity"></a>
<div id="cor:exact-complexity" class="corollary">

**Corollary 24** (Exact determinantal complexity). *For every integer $m\ge1408$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{dc}}(\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_m)\ge \frac{m^3}{5\,529\,600e}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-exact-complexity"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-exact-complexity</summary>

*Proof.* An exact determinant representation is also a constant sequence of approximating determinants. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(f)\le\mathop{\mathrm{dc}}(f)$ for every $f$, and Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-abp-complexity"></a>
<div id="cor:abp-complexity" class="corollary">

**Corollary 25** (Affine-linear algebraic branching programs). *Over $\mathbb C$, an algebraic branching program is a finite directed acyclic graph with distinct designated source $s$ and sink $t$, whose edges carry affine-linear forms; its output is the sum of the commutative products of labels along all directed $s$-to-$t$ paths. Such a program computing $\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_m$ requires $\Omega(m^3)$ vertices and $\Omega(m^3)$ edges, even after trimming to the vertices and edges on source-to-sink paths. The same bounds hold if $\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_m$ is a coefficientwise (equivalently, Zariski) limit of outputs with a fixed vertex budget, respectively a fixed edge budget, along the approximation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-abp-complexity"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-abp-complexity</summary>

*Proof.* Let a program with $N$ vertices have output $f$. Sum parallel-edge labels and topologically order the vertices. With adjacency convention $A_{v,u}=\text{label}(u\to v)$, the matrix $A$ is strictly triangular, $\det(I-A)=1$, and $(I-A)^{-1}=I+A+\cdots+A^{N-1}$. The path-sum formula and the block determinant identity give $$f=e_t^{\mathsf T}(I-A)^{-1}e_s
  =\det\begin{pmatrix}I-A&e_s\\-e_t^{\mathsf T}&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ This is an affine-linear determinant of size $N+1$. Padding with isolated vertices covers all programs with at most $N$ vertices. Taking closures therefore gives $\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(f)\le N+1$ for every output in the fixed-budget border model, even when the graph varies. After summing parallel edges, there are finitely many graph types for a fixed vertex budget, and each output depends polynomially on the affine-label coefficients. Hence the output set is constructible, so its two closures agree by the argument in Section <a href="#sec:models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:models">2</a>.

For a nonzero output, trim away all vertices and edges outside source-to-sink paths. The remaining underlying graph is connected, so $N\le E+1$, where $E$ is its edge count. Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(f)\le E+2$. For a fixed-edge-budget approximation to the nonzero permanent, discard any zero outputs and trim each remaining program separately; the same uniform determinantal bound passes to the limit. The finite graph-type argument also applies to this trimmed edge-budget model. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> now yields both cubic lower bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Smooth initial forms

Theorem <a href="#thm:smooth-border" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:smooth-border">4</a> also applies locally to a polynomial that need not be homogeneous. Its first nonzero homogeneous term can be obtained as a coefficientwise limit of translated and rescaled copies of that polynomial.

<a id="stmt-cor-smooth-initial-form"></a>
<div id="cor:smooth-initial-form" class="corollary">

**Corollary 26** (Smooth initial form). *Let $g\in\mathbb C[x_1,\ldots,x_d]$, where $d\ge2$, and let $a\in\mathbb C^d$. Suppose that the lowest nonzero homogeneous term $G_r(x)$ of $g(a+x)$ has degree $r\ge2$ and defines a smooth hypersurface in $\mathbb P^{d-1}$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(g)\ge\frac{(r-1)(d-1)}{4e}.$$ The same lower bound holds for $\mathop{\mathrm{dc}}(g)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-smooth-initial-form"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-smooth-initial-form</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a size $n$ for which $g$ belongs to the coefficientwise closure $\mathcal C_n$ of affine determinants of size $n$. Then $\deg g\le n$, and $\mathcal C_n$ is closed in the space of polynomials of degree at most $n$. For every fixed $t\in\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}$, Lemma <a href="#lem:affine-substitution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affine-substitution">2</a> gives $$t^{-r}g(a+tx)\in\mathcal C_n.$$ Writing $g(a+x)=\sum_{j=r}^{\deg g}G_j(x)$ as a sum of homogeneous terms, the polynomial on the left is $\sum_{j=r}^{\deg g}t^{j-r}G_j(x)$. It converges coefficientwise to $G_r$ as $t\to0$. Closedness therefore gives $G_r\in\mathcal C_n$. Theorem <a href="#thm:smooth-border" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:smooth-border">4</a> now bounds this same size $n$ below by $(r-1)(d-1)/(4e)$. Taking the least admissible $n$ proves the border assertion, and $\mathop{\mathrm{dc}}(g)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{\overline{dc}}}(g)$ proves the exact assertion. ◻

</details>

2q, \\quad T = \\binom{q+2}{2}$.\n- Down-left arrow from top box: Labeled \"$\\swarrow$ over $\\mathbb{C}$, every parameter choice\", pointing to middle-left box.\n- Down-right arrow from top box: Labeled \"$\\searrow$ over $K = \\mathbb{F}_2$, choose distinct parameters\", pointing to middle-right box.\n- Middle-left box: Labeled \"Permanent projection\", containing $\\operatorname{per}_m(A_m(X)) = a_0 G_D(X)$ and $m \\ge 11k - 2q, \\quad a_0 \\ne 0$.\n- Middle-right box: Labeled \"Plane-curve geometry\", containing $\\nabla G_{\\overline{D}}(X) = 0 \\implies \\delta(C_X) \\ge T$ and $\\operatorname{codim} V(\\nabla G_{\\overline{D}}) \\ge T - k$.\n- Down-right arrow from middle-left box: Labeled \"$\\searrow$ apply at the complex parameters supplied by geometry\", pointing to lower-middle box.\n- Down-left arrow from middle-right box: Labeled \"$\\swarrow$ proper specialization and a transverse linear section\", pointing to lower-middle box.\n- Lower-middle box: Labeled \"A smooth affine restriction over $\\mathbb{C}$\", containing text \"A nonzero homogeneous form $G$ of degree $r$ in $d$ variables has a smooth projective zero set and satisfies $G = \\operatorname{per}_m \\circ \\Phi_m$ for a fixed affine map $\\Phi_m$.\" and inequalities $r - 1)(d - 1)}{4e} \\ge \\frac{k^3}{3200e}$."
